Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output
17 8 86 92### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
   - Is as close as possible to (x,y) in Euclidean distance to its center
   - If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically smallest

## Mathematical Analysis

For a sub-rectangle with width w and height h, we need:
- w/h = a/b, which means w = (a/b) × h
- Since w and h must be integers, h must be a multiple of b, and w must be a multiple of a
- So let h = k × b, then w = k × a for some positive integer k

The sub-rectangle must contain (x,y), so:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m

With width w = k×a and height h = k×b, we have:
- x₂ - x₁ = k×a
- y₂ - y₁ = k×b

## Constraints on k

For the rectangle to fit within the grid:
- k×a ≤ n - x₁ + x₂, and since we want to contain (x,y), we need x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
- k×b ≤ m - y₁ + y₂, and since we want to contain (x,y), we need y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂

To maximize the area while ensuring the point is contained, we need to find the valid range of k values.

## Finding Valid k Values

For a given k:
- The width constraint: k×a ≤ n - x₁ + x₂
- The height constraint: k×b ≤ m - y₁ + y₂

Since we want the rectangle to contain (x,y), we can place it optimally:
- x₁ = x - w/2 = x - k×a/2
- x₂ = x + w/2 = x + k×a/2
- y₁ = y - h/2 = y - k×b/2  
- y₂ = y + h/2 = y + k×b/2

But these need to be integers, so in practice we need to ensure the boundaries are integers.

Actually, since we only require integer coordinates and the ratio a/b, we can work with the constraints directly.

4. If tied, choose lexicographically smallest

Algorithm:

1. k = min(n // a, m // b)
2. w = k * a
3. h = k * b
4. x1_valid = [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)]
5. y1_valid = [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)]
6. If x1_valid[0] > x1_valid[1] or y1_valid[0] > y1_valid[1], then no solution? 
   But from constraints, if k ≤ min(n//a, m//b), then w ≤ n, h ≤ m, and we should be able to place it.
   Actually, need to verify.
7. Find x1 in x1_valid that minimizes |x1 + w/2 - x|
8. Find y1 in y1_valid that minimizes |y1 + h/2 - y|
9. Return (x1, y1, x1 + w, y1 + h)

For the distance minimization:
- We want to minimize |x1 + w/2 - x| over x1 in range
- This is minimized when x1 is as close as possible to x - w/2
- So let target_x1 = x - w/2
- Choose x1 in x1_valid closest to target_x1
- Similarly for y1

But note: w, h may be odd, so w/2 may not be integer.

The center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2), which may not be integer.

For the distance, we care about Euclidean distance.

To find the closest value in an interval [low, high] to a target t:
- If t in [low, high], choose t
- Else if t < low, choose low
- Else if t > high, choose high

But since we have integers, and the target may not be integer.

We can do:

target_x1 = x - w/2
If target_x1 < x1_valid[0], choose x1_valid[0]
If target_x1 > x1_valid[1], choose x1_valid[1]
Else, choose floor(target_x1) or ceil(target_x1)? 

We want the integer x1 that minimizes |x1 + w/2 - x| = |x1 - (x - w/2)|

So we want x1 as close as possible to (x - w/2).

Since x1 is integer, the closest integer to a real number t is either floor(t) or ceil(t).

But if t is exactly halfway, we need to decide between floor and ceil.

In the problem, if multiple solutions have the same distance, we choose lexicographically smallest.

For lexicographical order, we want smaller x1 first, then y1.

So if there are ties in distance, choose the smaller value.

For x1, if target_x1 is in [x1_valid[0], x1_valid[1]], and we have two integers at the same distance, we choose the smaller one.

For example, if target_x1 = 2.5, and x1_valid = [1,3], then x1=2 and x1=3 are both at distance 0.5, we choose 2 (smaller).

So algorithm for x1:
- target = x - w/2
- If target < x1_valid[0], choose x1_valid[0]
- If target > x1_valid[1], choose x1_valid[1]
- Else, choose floor(target) if floor(target) in range, or ceil(target) if in range? 
- Actually, we want the closest integer to target in the interval.

We can compute:
- candidate1 = floor(target)
- candidate2 = ceil(target)
- If candidate1 in range and candidate2 in range, and |candidate1 - target| == |candidate2 - target|, then choose smaller (candidate1)
- Otherwise, choose the one in range that is closer.

But |candidate1 - target| == |candidate2 - target| only if target is halfway between two integers.

In that case, |target - floor(target)| = |target - ceil(target)| = 0.5.

So if target is halfway, we have two candidates at same distance.

Then we choose the smaller one (floor).

Otherwise, one is closer.

So:
- if target is in [x1_valid[0], x1_valid[1]], then we choose floor(target) if it's in range, else choose ceil(target)
- But floor(target) might be outside the range.

Better:
- candidates = []
- if x1_valid[0] <= target <= x1_valid[1]:
  - candidates.append(floor(target))
  - candidates.append(ceil(target))
- else:
  - if target < x1_valid[0], add x1_valid[0]
  - if target > x1_valid[1], add x1_valid[1]

But since the distance is minimized, and if target is in range, floor(target) and ceil(target) are both in range only if the interval is wide enough.

Actually, if target is in [low, high], then floor(target) >= low (since target >= low), and floor(target) <= high - 1 (if target < high), but not necessarily in range.

For example, if target=3.2, low=3, high=4, then floor=3, ceil=4, both in range.

If target=3.1, low=3, high=4, floor=3, ceil=4, both in range.

The only case where floor(target) might be less than low is when target < low, which we already handle.

Similarly for ceil.

So if target < low, only low is valid.
If target > high, only high is valid.
If low <= target <= high, then floor(target) >= low (since target >= low and floor(target) >= target - 1), but floor(target) might be < low.

For example, target=3.0, low=3.0, high=5.0: floor=3.0, in range.
target=3.1, low=3.0, high=5.0: floor=3, in range.

Actually, if target >= low, then floor(target) >= floor(low) >= low, since low is integer.

Similarly, if target <= high, then ceil(target) <= ceil(high) <= high.

So if target in [low, high] and low, high are integers, then floor(target) >= low and ceil(target) <= high.

So both floor and ceil are in range only if low <= target <= high.

And if target is not halfway, one is closer.

If target is halfway, both are at same distance, and we choose the smaller one.

So algorithm:
- target_x1 = x - w/2
- if target_x1 < x1_valid[0]:
  - x1 = x1_valid[0]
- elif target_x1 > x1_valid[1]:
  - x1 = x1_valid[1]
- else:
  - target_int = int(target_x1)  # floor
  - if abs(target_x1 - target_int) < abs(target_x1 - (target_int + 1)):
    - x1 = target_int
  - else:
    - x1 = target_int + 1  # when exactly halfway, we want smaller? no

For halfway: target_x1 = c + 0.5 for integer c.

Then |target_x1 - c| = 0.5, |target_x1 - (c+1)| = 0.5, same distance.

We want lexicographically smallest, so we want smaller x1.

So if equally distant, choose c.

In this case, target_int is c, so we choose target_int.

If target_x1 is not halfway, we choose the closer one.

But if target_x1 < target_int + 1 and target_x1 > target_int, and not halfway, the closer one is target_int.

For example, target_x1=3.2, closer to 3.
target_x1=3.7, closer to 4.

So:
- if target_x1 - target_int < 0.5, choose target_int
- else if target_x1 - target_int > 0.5, choose target_int + 1
- else (exactly 0.5), choose target_int

But target_int is floor(target_x1), so:
- if target_x1 - floor(target_x1) < 0.5, choose floor
- else, choose floor + 1

This ensures we get the closest integer to target_x1.

And when exactly 0.5, we choose floor, which is smaller.

Now for y1 similarly.

Then return (x1, y1, x1 + w, y1 + h)

We can write w = k×a, h = k×b for any k>0.

For k=1: w=23, h=28
k=2: w=46, h=56
k=3: w=69, h=84
k=4: w=92, h=112 > 100, invalid

So valid k values: 1,2,3

k_max = min(n//a, m//b) where a,b are reduced, but we can reduce a and b.

We need to find the greatest common divisor.

Let g = gcd(a,b)
a' = a // g, b' = b // g
Then any rectangle with ratio a/b has dimensions w = k × a', h = k × b' for some positive integer k.

And the ratio w/h = a'/b' = a/b.

So we need to find k such that:
- k × a' ≤ n, so k ≤ n // a'
- k × b' ≤ m, so k ≤ m // b'
- And can be placed to contain (x,y)

k_max = min(n // a', m // b')

For the example: a=46, b=56
g = gcd(46,56) = 2
a' = 23, b' = 28
k_max = min(100 // 23, 100 // 28) = min(4,3) = 3

So final algorithm:

1. Read n, m, x, y, a, b
2. g = gcd(a, b)
3. a_prime = a // g
4. b_prime = b // g
5. k_max = min(n // a_prime, m // b_prime)
6. w = k_max * a_prime
7. h = k_max * b_prime
8. x1_valid = [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)]
9. y1_valid = [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)]
10. If x1_valid[0] > x1_valid[1] or y1_valid[0] > y1_valid[1], then no solution? But should have one.
11. target_x1 = x - w/2
12. if target_x1 < x1_valid[0]:
13.     x1 = x1_valid[0]
14. elif target_x1 > x1_valid[1]:
15.     x1 = x1_valid[1]
16. else:
17.     x1 = floor(target_x1)   # because when halfway, we want smaller
18. target_y1 = y - h/2
19. if target_y1 < y1_valid[0]:
20.     y1 = y1_valid[0]
21. elif target_y1 > y1_valid[1]:
22.     y1 = y1_valid[1]
23. else:
24.     y1 = floor(target_y1)
25. x2 = x1 + w
26. y2 = y1 + h
27. print(x1, y1, x2, y2)

Note: floor in Python for float can be done with int() or math.floor, but for negative we need care. Since target_x1 is likely non-negative, it's safe.

Actually, target_x1 = x - w/2, w = k×a', which is positive, x >=0, so target_x1 could be negative.

But when negative, we already have x1_valid[0] >=0, so if target_x1 < 0, we choose x1_valid[0].

In the "else" case, target_x1 is in [x1_valid[0], x1_valid[1]], and since x1_valid[0] >=0, target_x1 >=0.

So floor is safe.

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Reduce a and b by gcd
    g = math.gcd(a, b)
    a_prime = a // g
    b_prime = b // g
    
    # Maximum k such that the rectangle fits
    k_max = min(n // a_prime, m // b_prime)
    
    # Dimensions of the rectangle
    w = k_max * a_prime
    h = k_max * b_prime
    
    # Valid range for x1 and y1 to contain (x,y)
    x1_low = max(0, x - w)
    x1_high = min(x, n - w)
    
    y1_low = max(0, y - h)
    y1_high = min(y, m - h)
    
    # If no valid range, this shouldn't happen with our constraints
    if x1_low > x1_high or y1_low > y1_high:
        # Fallback: try smaller k, but since we want maximum area, this should not occur
        # For safety, use k=1
        k = 1
        w = a_prime
        h = b_prime
        x1_low = max(0, x - w)
        x1_high = min(x, n - w)
        y1_low = max(0, y - h)
        y1_high = min(y, m - h)
        if x1_low > x1_high or y1_low > y1_high:
            # Even with k=1, no solution? Then just place as small as possible
            x1 = 0
            y1 = 0
            x2 = w
            y2 = h
        else:
            target_x1 = x - w / 2
            if target_x1 < x1_low:
                x1 = x1_low
            elif target_x1 > x1_high:
                x1 = x1_high
            else:
                x1 = int(target_x1)
            target_y1 = y - h / 2
            if target_y1 < y1_low:
                y1 = y1_low
            elif target_y1 > y1_high:
                y1 = y1_high
            else:
                y1 = int(target_y1)
    else:
        target_x1 = x - w / 2
        if target_x1 < x1_low:
            x1 = x1_low
        elif target_x1 > x1_high:
            x1 = x1_high
        else:
            x1 = int(target_x1)
        
        target_y1 = y - h / 2
        if target_y1 < y1_low:
            y1 = y1_low
        elif target_y1 > y1_high:
            y1 = y1_high
        else:
            y1 = int(target_y1)
    
    x2 = x1 + w
    y2 = y1 + h
    print(x1, y1, x2, y2)

solve()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

100 100 52 50 46 56

Output

Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a sub-rectangle in an n×m grid that:
1. Contains the point (x, y)
2. Has length-width ratio exactly a/b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
3. Is as close as possible to (x, y) in Euclidean distance
4. If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. We need (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b, which means (x₂-x₁) = k×a and (y₂-y₁) = k×b for some positive integer k.

2. The rectangle must contain (x, y), so:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂

3. We can express the rectangle dimensions as:
   - width = k×a
   - height = k×b

4. For a fixed k, we need to find valid boundaries:
   - x₁ can range from 0 to x (since x₁ ≤ x)
   - x₂ = x₁ + k×a must be ≤ n and ≥ x
   - y₁ can range from 0 to y (since y₁ ≤ y)
   - y₂ = y₁ + k×b must be ≤ m and ≥ y

5. For each valid k, we can find the optimal placement that minimizes distance to (x, y).

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Find valid values of k**: 
   - k×a + x₁ ≤ n, so x₁ ≤ n - k×a
   - x₁ ≥ 0, so k×a ≤ n
   - Similarly, k×b ≤ m
   
   So k must satisfy: k ≤ min(n/a, m/b)

2. **For each valid k, find optimal boundaries**:
   - We want to minimize the distance from (x, y) to the center of the rectangle
   - Center of rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁+x₁+k×a)/2, (y₁+y₁+k×b)/2)
   - Distance = √[((x₁ + k×a - 2x)/2)² + ((y₁ + k×b - 2y)/2)²]
   - To minimize distance, we want (x₁ + k×a - 2x) and (y₁ + k×b - 2y) to be as close to 0 as possible

3. **Constraints for boundaries**:
   - 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x
   - x₁ + k×a ≥ x (so x₂ ≥ x)
   - x₁ + k×a ≤ n (so x₂ ≤ n)
   - 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y  
   - y₁ + k×b ≥ y (so y₂ ≥ y)
   - y₁ + k×b ≤ m (so y₂ ≤ m)

## Algorithm

For each valid k from 1 to min(n//a, m//b):
- Find valid x₁ range: max(0, x - k×a) to min(x, n - k×a)
- Find valid y₁ range: max(0, y - k×b) to min(y, m - k×b)
- For each valid (x₁, y₁), calculate center distance
- Choose the one with minimum distance, then lexicographically smallest if tie

Wait, let me optimize this. Since we want to minimize distance, for each k we can:
- For x₁: we want x₁ + k×a ≈ 2x, so x₁ ≈ 2x - k×a
- For y₁: we want y₁ + k×b ≈ 2y, so y₁ ≈ 2y - k×b

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find valid range for k
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_rect = None
    best_dist = float('inf')
    
    # Try different values of k
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Find valid x1 range
        # x1 <= x (since x1 <= x)
        # x1 + width >= x (so x2 >= x)
        # x1 + width <= n (so x2 <= n)
        
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)  # x1 + width >= x
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)  # x1 <= x and x1 + width <= n
        
        # Find valid y1 range
        # y1 <= y (since y1 <= y)
        # y1 + height >= y (so y2 >= y)
        # y1 + height <= m (so y2 <= m)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)  # y1 + height >= y
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)  # y1 <= y and y1 + height <= m
        
        # If no valid range, skip
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Find optimal x1 and y1 for this k
        # We want to minimize distance to center (x, y)
        # Center is at ((x1 + x1 + width)/2, (y1 + y1 + height)/2) = (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
        
        # Distance squared: (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
        # To minimize, we want x1 + width/2 ≈ x and y1 + height/2 ≈ y
        # So x1 ≈ x - width/2 and y1 ≈ y - height/2
        
        # Check the closest valid values for x1 and y1
        x1_opt = int(round(x - width / 2))
        y1_opt = int(round(y - height / 2))
        
        # But we need to clamp to valid ranges
        x1_candidate = x1_opt
        y1_candidate = y1_opt
        
        # Try the best possible values in the valid ranges
        candidates = []
        
        # Try the rounded values if they're in range
        if x1_min <= x1_opt <= x1_max:
            candidates.append((x1_opt, y1_opt))
        
        # Also try adjacent integers
        for dx in [-1, 0, 1]:
            for dy in [-1, 0, 1]:
                x1_t = x1_opt + dx
                y1_t = y1_opt + dy
                if x1_min <= x1_t <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_t <= y1_max:
                    candidates.append((x1_t, y1_t))
        
        # Remove duplicates and sort
        candidates = list(set(candidates))
        candidates.sort()
        
        for x1, y1 in candidates:
            x2 = x1 + width
            y2 = y1 + height
            
            # Calculate center distance
            center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
            center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
            dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            if dist_sq < best_dist:
                best_dist = dist_sq
                best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
            elif dist_sq == best_dist:
                # Lexicographically smaller
                if (x1, y1, x2, y2) < best_rect:
                    best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
    
    print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    best_rect = None
    best_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try each possible k value
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Find valid x1 range
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)  # x1 + width >= x
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)  # x1 <= x and x1 + width <= n
        
        # Find valid y1 range  
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)  # y1 + height >= y
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)  # y1 <= y and y1 + height <= m
        
        # If no valid range, skip
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, find the optimal (x1, y1) to minimize distance
        # We want to minimize (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
        # So x1 should be close to x - width/2, y1 should be close to y - height/2
        
        ideal_x1 = x - width / 2
        ideal_y1 = y - height / 2
        
        # Try values around the ideal
        candidates = []
        
        # Add candidates in the valid range
        for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
            for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
                x2 = x1 + width
                y2 = y1 + height
                center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
                center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
                dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                
                # If we found a better one, keep it
                if dist_sq < best_dist_squared:
                    best_dist_squared = dist_sq
                    best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
                elif dist_sq == best_dist_squared:
                    # Lexicographically smaller
                    if (x1, y1, x2, y2) < best_rect:
                        best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
        
        # Early termination: if k is very large, will likely not improve
        # But we need to check all k since we don't know
        
    print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])
```

```python
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)

        continue

    ideal_x1 = x - width / 2
    ideal_y1 = y - height / 2

    candidates = []

        x1_candidate = int(ideal_x1) + d
        if x1_min <= x1_candidate <= x1_max:
            # Try y1 values around ideal_y1

                y1_candidate = int(ideal_y1) + e
                if y1_min <= y1_candidate <= y1_max:

if best_rect is None:
    # Fallback: just use the first valid rectangle
    width = a
    height = b
    x1 = max(0, x - width)
    y1 = max(0, y - height)
    x2 = x1 + width
    y2 = y1 + height
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output
17 8 86 92### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a sub-rectangle in an n×m grid that:
1. Contains a given point (x, y)
2. Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
3. Is as close as possible to the center of the original rectangle
4. If multiple solutions exist, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. For a rectangle with width w = x₂ - x₁ and height h = y₂ - y₁, we need w/h = a/b
2. This means w = k×a and h = k×b for some positive integer k
3. The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁ + x₂)/2, (y₁ + y₂)/2)
4. We want to minimize the Euclidean distance from (x, y) to this center
5. If distance is equal, we want lexicographically smallest (x₁, y₁, x₂, y₂)

## Solution Steps

1. For each possible scaling factor k, we can compute possible dimensions
2. For a given k, we have width = k×a, height = k×b
3. The sub-rectangle must contain (x, y), so:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a → x₁ ≥ max(0, x - k×a) and x₁ ≤ min(n - k×a, x)
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b → y₁ ≥ max(0, y - k×b) and y₁ ≤ min(m - k×b, y)
4. For each valid k, we find the best (x₁, y₁) that minimizes distance from center
5. Among all valid solutions, we pick the one that's closest to (x, y), then lexicographically smallest

## Optimization

Instead of checking all possible k values, we note that:
- The maximum k is bounded by min(n/a, m/b)
- We can iterate through reasonable values of k

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # For a rectangle with ratio a/b, we need width = k*a, height = k*b
    # The center is at ((x1+x2)/2, (y1+y2)/2) = ((x1 + x1 + k*a)/2, (y1 + y1 + k*b)/2)
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    min_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Find maximum possible k
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b) if a > 0 and b > 0 else 0
    
    # Try different values of k
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Find valid range for x1
        # x1 <= x <= x1 + width
        # So x1 >= max(0, x - width) and x1 <= min(n - width, x)
        min_x1 = max(0, x - width)
        max_x1 = min(n - width, x)
        
        # Find valid range for y1  
        # y1 <= y <= y1 + height
        # So y1 >= max(0, y - height) and y1 <= min(m - height, y)
        min_y1 = max(0, y - height)
        max_y1 = min(m - height, y)
        
        # If no valid range, skip this k
        if min_x1 > max_x1 or min_y1 > max_y1:
            continue
            
        # Try all combinations in valid ranges
        # To minimize distance and get lexicographically smallest,
        # we need to check the valid ranges
        
        # For the center to be as close as possible to (x, y),
        # we want the center to be as close as possible to (x, y)
        # The center is at (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
        
        # Try to find optimal x1 and y1 values
        # For fixed k, we want to minimize distance squared
        # distance^2 = (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
        
        # We can iterate through possible x1 values and find best y1
        for x1 in range(min_x1, max_x1 + 1):
            # For this x1, center_x = x1 + width/2
            center_x = x1 + width / 2.0
            center_y = None
            
            for y1 in range(min_y1, max_y1 + 1):
                center_y = y1 + height / 2.0
                dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                
                x2 = x1 + width
                y2 = y1 + height
                
                if dist_sq < min_distance_squared:
                    min_distance_squared = dist_sq
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                elif dist_sq == min_distance_squared:
                    # Check lexicographic order
                    current = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
                    best_current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                    if current < best_current:
                        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                        
    # If no solution found (shouldn't happen given constraints), use default
    if best_x1 is None:
        # This should not happen with valid inputs
        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, a, b
        
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's an issue with the approach. The range of k might be too large (up to 10^9). Let me optimize this.

Actually, I need to be more careful. The ratio must be exactly a/b, so for each possible width = k*a and height = k*b, we need to check valid positions.

But the key insight is that we only need to check a reasonable number of k values. Since the maximum ratio is bounded, and we want to minimize distance to (x,y), there will be only a few values of k that actually produce rectangles close to the optimal solution.

Let me reconsider - the constraints are large, so I need to be more efficient.

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    min_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Maximum value of k is limited by the dimensions
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b) if a > 0 and b > 0 else 0
    
    # For each possible k, we try to find the best position
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Valid range for x1: must contain x
        min_x1 = max(0, x - width)
        max_x1 = min(n - width, x)
        
        # Valid range for y1: must contain y  
        min_y1 = max(0, y - height)
        max_y1 = min(m - height, y)
        
        # If no valid range, skip
        if min_x1 > max_x1 or min_y1 > max_y1:
            continue
            
        # For this k, we want to find x1, y1 that minimize distance to (x, y)
        # Distance squared = (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
        # To minimize this, we want x1 close to x - width/2 and y1 close to y - height/2
        
        # Find the optimal x1 and y1 values within bounds
        # The ideal x1 would be x - width/2, but we constrain it to [min_x1, max_x1]
        ideal_x1 = x - width / 2.0
        actual_x1 = int(round(ideal_x1))
        
        # Check bounds and nearby values
        candidates_x1 = []
        for dx in range(-1, 2):
            cand_x1 = int(ideal_x1 + dx)
            if min_x1 <= cand_x1 <= max_x1:
                candidates_x1.append(cand_x1)
        
        # Similarly for y1
        ideal_y1 = y - height / 2.0
        actual_y1 = int(round(ideal_y1))
        
        candidates_y1 = []
        for dy in range(-1, 2):
            cand_y1 = int(ideal_y1 + dy)
            if min_y1 <= cand_y1 <= max_y1:
                candidates_y1.append(cand_y1)
        
        # Also add the boundaries to be safe
        for cand_x1 in [min_x1, max_x1]:
            if min_x1 <= cand_x1 <= max_x1:
                candidates_x1.append(cand_x1)
                
        for cand_y1 in [min_y1, max_y1]:
            if min_y1 <= cand_y1 <= max_y1:
                candidates_y1.append(cand_y1)
                
        # Remove duplicates and sort
        candidates_x1 = list(set(candidates_x1))
        candidates_y1 = list(set(candidates_y1))
        
        # Filter valid candidates
        valid_candidates = []
        for x1 in candidates_x1:
            if min_x1 <= x1 <= max_x1:
                for y1 in candidates_y1:
                    if min_y1 <= y1 <= max_y1:
                        x2 = x1 + width
                        y2 = y1 + height
                        dist_sq = (x1 + width/2 - x)**2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)**2
                        valid_candidates.append((dist_sq, x1, y1, x2, y2))
        
        # Sort by distance, then by lexicographic order
        valid_candidates.sort()
        
        for dist_sq, x1, y1, x2, y2 in valid_candidates:
            if dist_sq < min_distance_squared:
                min_distance_squared = dist_sq
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
            elif dist_sq == min_distance_squared:
                # Check lexicographic order
                if (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2):
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
    
    # If no solution found, use default
    if best_x1 is None:
        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, a, b
        
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
min_distance_squared = float('inf')

# Try a reasonable range of k values
max_k = min(n // a, m // b) if a > 0 and b > 0 else 0

# We only need to check up to a reasonable limit
# Since we want rectangles close to (x,y), we can limit k
# The maximum k we need to check is when the rectangle size is reasonable

# Try k from 1 to min(n//a, m//b)
# But limit to avoid TLE
max_k_to_check = min(max_k, 100000)  # Reasonable limit

for k in range(1, max_k_to_check + 1):
    width = k * a
    height = k * b
    
    # Check if this rectangle can contain (x,y)
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(n - width, x)
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(m - height, y)
    
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
        
    # Find the best x1 and y1 that minimize distance to center
    # Center is at (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
    # Distance squared = (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
    
    # Ideal x1: x - width/2
    ideal_x1 = x - width / 2.0
    ideal_y1 = y - height / 2.0
    
    # Check values around the ideal
    for x1 in [int(ideal_x1 - 0.5), int(ideal_x1), int(ideal_x1 + 0.5)]:
        if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max:
            for y1 in [int(ideal_y1 - 0.5), int(ideal_y1), int(ideal_y1 + 0.5)]:
                if y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                    x2 = x1 + width
                    y2 = y1 + height
                    dist_sq = (x1 + width/2 - x)**2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)**2
                    
                    if dist_sq < min_distance_squared:
                        min_distance_squared = dist_sq
                        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                    elif dist_sq == min_distance_squared:
                        # Check lexicographic order
                        if (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2):
                            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

if best_x1 is None:
    # Fallback - should not happen
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, a, b

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

    # Find the best x1 and y1 that minimize distance to center
    # Center is at (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
    # Distance squared = (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
    
    # Ideal x1: x - width/2
    ideal_x1 = x - width / 2.0
    ideal_y1 = y - height / 2.0
    
    # Check values around the ideal
    for x1 in [int(ideal_x1 - 0.5), int(ideal_x1), int(ideal_x1 + 0.5)]:
        if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max:
            for y1 in [int(ideal_y1 - 0.5), int(ideal_y1), int(ideal_y1 + 0.5)]:
                if y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                    x2 = x1 + width
                    y2 = y1 + height
                    dist_sq = (x1 + width/2 - x)**2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)**2
                    
                    if dist_sq < min_distance_squared:
                        min_distance_squared = dist_sq
                        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                    elif dist_sq == min_distance_squared:
                        # Check lexicographic order
                        if (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2):
                            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

if best_x1 is None:
    # Fallback - should not happen
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, a, b

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0≤x≤n, 0≤y≤m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
   - Is closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance (if multiple)
   - If still multiple, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b
2. This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)
3. We can express the width (w = x₂-x₁) and height (h = y₂-y₁) such that w/h = a/b
4. This means w = k*a and h = k*b for some positive integer k

## Solution Approach

1. For the sub-rectangle to contain (x,y), we need:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂

2. Given width w = k*a and height h = k*b, we have:
   - x₁ = x - w/2 (centered around x)
   - x₂ = x + w/2 (centered around x)
   - y₁ = y - h/2 (centered around y)
   - y₂ = y + h/2 (centered around y)

3. We need to find valid k values such that:
   - x₁ ≥ 0 and x₂ ≤ n
   - y₁ ≥ 0 and y₂ ≤ m

4. For each valid k, we get a candidate rectangle. We need to:
   - Check if it's valid (within bounds)
   - Calculate Euclidean distance from (x,y) to rectangle center
   - Choose the best one

5. The center of rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = (x, y) since it's centered.

Wait, let me reconsider. Since the rectangle must contain (x,y), and we want it to be "closest" to (x,y), we should center it at (x,y).

If we center at (x,y), then:
- x₁ = x - w/2, x₂ = x + w/2
- y₁ = y - h/2, y₂ = y + h/2

For integer coordinates, we need w and h to be such that w/2 and h/2 are either integers or we can adjust.

Actually, since w = k*a and h = k*b, we need k*a and k*b to be such that the centering works.

For the rectangle to have integer coordinates and contain (x,y), we need:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂

With central positioning: x₁ = x - w/2, x₂ = x + w/2, etc.

So constraints:
- x - w/2 ≥ 0 ⇒ w ≤ 2x
- x + w/2 ≤ n ⇒ w ≤ 2(n-x)
- y - h/2 ≥ 0 ⇒ h ≤ 2y  
- y + h/2 ≤ m ⇒ h ≤ 2(m-y)

Since w = k*a and h = k*b, we get:
- k*a ≤ 2x
- k*a ≤ 2(n-x)
- k*b ≤ 2y
- k*b ≤ 2(m-y)

So k ≤ min(2x/a, 2(n-x)/a, 2y/b, 2(m-y)/b)

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating. The distance is always 0 for centered rectangles, so we just pick the lexicographically smallest valid one.

But the problem says "closest to (x,y)" - since all centered rectangles have the same distance, it's just lexicographic.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum k such that the rectangle fits
max_k = min(2 * x // a if a > 0 else float('inf'), 
            2 * (n - x) // a if a > 0 else float('inf'),
            2 * y // b if b > 0 else float('inf'),
            2 * (m - y) // b if b > 0 else float('inf'))

# If max_k is still infinite, it means a or b is 0, but given constraints a>=1, b>=1
if max_k == float('inf'):
    max_k = 0

# Try all k from 1 to max_k, and find the lexicographically smallest valid rectangle
best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None

for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Calculate coordinates if centered at (x,y)
    x1 = x - w // 2
    x2 = x + w // 2
    y1 = y - h // 2
    y2 = y + h // 2
    
    # Check bounds
    if x1 < 0 or x2 > n or y1 < 0 or y2 > m:
        continue
    
    # This rectangle is valid. We want lexicographically smallest
    if best_x1 is None or (x1 < best_x1 or 
                          (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1) or 
                          (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 < best_x2) or 
                          (x1 == best_x1 and y1 == best_y1 and x2 == best_x2 and y2 < best_y2)):
        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

# If no valid rectangle found, try k=1 (should always work for non-trivial cases)
if best_x1 is None:
    w = a
    h = b
    x1 = x - w // 2
    x2 = x + w // 2
    y1 = y - h // 2
    y2 = y + h // 2
    if x1 < 0 or x2 > n or y1 < 0 or y2 > m:
        # This shouldn't happen with valid input
        x1, x2 = 0, n
        y1, y2 = 0, m
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0≤x≤n, 0≤y≤m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is aligned with axes
   - Has integer coordinates
3. Among all valid rectangles, we want:
   - Closest to point (x,y) by Euclidean distance to center
   - If tie, lexicographically smallest (x₁,y₁,x₂,y₂)

## Mathematical Analysis

Given the ratio requirement: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b
This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

Let the width w = y₂-y₁, then length l = (a/b) × w
Since l must be integer, w must be divisible by b, so w = k×b for some positive integer k
Then l = (a/b) × (k×b) = a×k

So we have:
- Width: k×b
- Length: k×a

We also need the rectangle to contain (x,y), so:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂

This gives us constraints:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a×k
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b×k

## Finding Valid k values

From x constraint: x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a×k
Since x₁ ≥ 0: x ≤ x₁ + a×k ≤ n
So x ≤ n and x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a×k

From x₂ constraint: x₂ = x₁ + a×k ≤ n
So x₁ + a×k ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - a×k

From y constraint: y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b×k
Since y₁ ≥ 0: y ≤ y₁ + b×k ≤ m
So y ≤ m and y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b×k

From y₂ constraint: y₂ = y₁ + b×k ≤ m
So y₁ + b×k ≤ m → y₁ ≤ m - b×k

## Finding k range

For given k:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a×k
- x₁ ≤ n - a×k

So we need x₁ ≤ min(x, n - a×k)

From y constraint:
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b×k
- y₁ ≤ m - b×k

So we need y₁ ≤ min(y, m - b×k)

For valid k, we need:
- a×k ≤ n (so x₁ ≥ 0 and x₂ ≤ n)
- b×k ≤ m (so y₁ ≥ 0 and y₂ ≤ m)
- x₁ ≤ x and x₁ ≤ n - a×k
- y₁ ≤ y and y₁ ≤ m - b×k

The maximum possible k is limited by: k ≤ min(n//a, m//b)

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all valid k values (from 1 to min(n//a, m//b))
2. For each k, find valid ranges for x₁ and y₁
3. For each valid rectangle, calculate distance to center and compare
4. Return the best one

But since n,m can be up to 10^9, we can't iterate over all k values.

Instead, we need to find which k values give valid rectangles and determine the optimal one.

## Key Insight

For a fixed k, the constraints are:
- x₁ ≤ x and x₁ ≤ n - a×k → x₁ ≤ min(x, n - a×k)
- y₁ ≤ y and y₁ ≤ m - b×k → y₁ ≤ min(y, m - b×k)

We also need x₁ ≥ 0, y₁ ≥ 0.

So for valid k:
- x₁ ∈ [0, min(x, n - a×k)]
- y₁ ∈ [0, min(y, m - b×k)]

For each such rectangle, the center is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + a×k)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + b×k)/2) = ((2x₁ + a×k)/2, (2y₁ + b×k)/2)

Distance squared to (x,y): (x - (2x₁ + a×k)/2)² + (y - (2y₁ + b×k)/2)²

To minimize this, we want:
- x₁ as close to (x - a×k/2) as possible
- y₁ as close to (y - b×k/2) as possible

For fixed k, optimal x₁ is min(x, n - a×k) (closest to the target), and optimal y₁ is min(y, m - b×k).

Actually, we want x₁ to maximize (x - x₁) and minimize distance.

The distance is minimized when x₁ is as close as possible to x - a×k/2, but constrained by 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ min(x, n - a×k).

So optimal x₁ is:
- If x - a×k/2 ≤ 0, then x₁ = 0
- If x - a×k/2 ≥ min(x, n - a×k), then x₁ = min(x, n - a×k)
- Otherwise, x₁ = round(x - a×k/2) but considering constraints

But we need to handle the lexicographic ordering.

Let me simplify: for each valid k, we can find the best (x₁,y₁) that minimizes distance to center.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    min_distance_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible k values from 1 to max_k
    # But since n,m can be large, we need to be smart about this
    # Actually, the solution will likely involve a specific k range
    
    # For each k from 1 to max_k
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        # Width is b*k, length is a*k
        w = b * k
        l = a * k
        
        # Constraints for x1 and x2
        # x1 <= x <= x1 + l, so x1 <= x and x1 <= n - l
        # x1 >= 0, x2 = x1 + l <= n
        x1_max = min(x, n - l)
        if x1_max < 0:
            continue
            
        # Constraints for y1 and y2  
        # y1 <= y <= y1 + w, so y1 <= y and y1 <= m - w
        # y1 >= 0, y2 = y1 + w <= m
        y1_max = min(y, m - w)
        if y1_max < 0:
            continue
            
        # For this k, find best x1, y1 to minimize distance to center
        # Center is at ((x1 + x1 + l)/2, (y1 + y1 + w)/2) = ((2*x1 + l)/2, (2*y1 + w)/2)
        # Distance squared to (x,y): (x - (2*x1 + l)/2)^2 + (y - (2*y1 + w)/2)^2
        
        # We want to minimize: (x - (2*x1 + l)/2)^2 + (y - (2*y1 + w)/2)^2
        # This is equivalent to minimizing: (2*x - (2*x1 + l))^2/4 + (2*y - (2*y1 + w))^2/4
        # So minimize: (2*x - 2*x1 - l)^2 + (2*y - 2*y1 - w)^2
        # = 4*(x - x1 - l/2)^2 + 4*(y - y1 - w/2)^2
        
        # For fixed k, best x1 is the one that makes x - x1 - l/2 as close to 0 as possible
        # So x1 should be as close as possible to (x - l/2)
        
        target_x1 = x - l / 2
        if target_x1 < 0:
            x1_opt = 0
        elif target_x1 > x1_max:
            x1_opt = x1_max
        else:
            # x1_opt should be the integer closest to target_x1
            # But we need to check if it's within bounds
            x1_opt = int(round(target_x1))
            if x1_opt < 0:
                x1_opt = 0
            elif x1_opt > x1_max:
                x1_opt = x1_max
            else:
                # Check if we should choose floor or ceil
                # For lexicographic ordering, we might need to be careful
                # But let's just take rounded value
                pass
                
        target_y1 = y - w / 2
        if target_y1 < 0:
            y1_opt = 0
        elif target_y1 > y1_max:
            y1_opt = y1_max
        else:
            y1_opt = int(round(target_y1))
            if y1_opt < 0:
                y1_opt = 0
            elif y1_opt > y1_max:
                y1_opt = y1_max
        
        # But we need to ensure x1_opt and y1_opt are within bounds
        x1_opt = max(0, min(x1_opt, x1_max))
        y1_opt = max(0, min(y1_opt, y1_max))
        
        # Calculate center distance squared
        center_x = (2 * x1_opt + l) / 2
        center_y = (2 * y1_opt + w) / 2
        dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
        
        # Check if this is better than current best
        if dist_sq < min_distance_sq:
            min_distance_sq = dist_sq
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + l, y1_opt + w
        elif dist_sq == min_distance_sq:
            # Lexicographic comparison
            current = (x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + l, y1_opt + w)
            best_current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
            if current < best_current:
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + l, y1_opt + w
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

Wait, this might be too slow for large k values. Let me reconsider.

Since n, m can be up to 10^9, and k goes up to min(n//a, m//b), which could be 10^9, we can't iterate over all k.

I need to optimize further. The key insight is that the optimal solution likely occurs for a specific range of k values.

Actually, let me check the constraints again. For the rectangle to contain (x,y), we need:
- x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + a*k
- y1 ≤ y ≤ y1 + b*k

The best center is when the center is as close as possible to (x,y). The center is at ((2*x1 + a*k)/2, (2*y1 + b*k)/2).

To minimize distance, we want:
- (2*x1 + a*k)/2 ≈ x → x1 ≈ x - a*k/2
- (2*y1 + b*k)/2 ≈ y → y1 ≈ y - b*k/2

For x1 to be valid: 0 ≤ x1 ≤ min(x, n - a*k)
So we need: 0 ≤ x - a*k/2 ≤ min(x, n - a*k)

This gives constraints on k.

Similarly for y.

But still, k can be large. Let me try a different approach - check only reasonable k values.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

def distance_sq(x1, y1, k):
    l = a * k
    w = b * k
    x2 = x1 + l
    y2 = y1 + w
    if x1 < 0 or x2 > n or y1 < 0 or y2 > m:
        return float('inf')
    center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
    center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
    return (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2

max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, 0, 0
min_dist_sq = float('inf')

# Check all k values from 1 to max_k
# But we can skip some values - only check k where the center is reasonably close
for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = b * k
    l = a * k
    
    # Find valid x1 range: 0 <= x1 <= min(x, n - l)
    x1_max = min(x, n - l)
    if x1_max < 0:
        continue
        
    # Find valid y1 range: 0 <= y1 <= min(y, m - w)
    y1_max = min(y, m - w)
    if y1_max < 0:
        continue
        
    # For each valid (x1,y1), compute distance
    # To minimize distance, choose x1 and y1 close to their optimal values
    target_x1 = x - l / 2
    target_y1 = y - w / 2
    
    x1_opt = max(0, min(int(target_x1 + 0.5), x1_max))
    y1_opt = max(0, min(int(target_y1 + 0.5), y1_max))
    
    # Also check nearby values for lexicographic order
    candidates = []
    
    # Add the optimal candidates
    candidates.append((x1_opt, y1_opt))
    
    # Add boundary candidates to ensure lexicographic correctness
    if target_x1 >= 0:
        candidates.append((0, y1_opt))
        candidates.append((x1_max, y1_opt))
    if target_y1 >= 0:
        candidates.append((x1_opt, 0))
        candidates.append((x1_opt, y1_max))
    
    for x1, y1 in candidates:
        x2 = x1 + l
        y2 = y1 + w
        if x1 < 0 or x2 > n or y1 < 0 or y2 > m:
            continue
        dist_sq = (x - (x1 + x2) / 2) ** 2 + (y - (y1 + y2) / 2) ** 2
        if dist_sq < min_dist_sq:
            min_dist_sq = dist_sq
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
        elif dist_sq == min_dist_sq:
            # Compare lexicographically
            current = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
            best_current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
            if current < best_current:
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

Let me think differently. The optimal solution occurs when k is such that the center is as close as possible to (x,y). This happens when x1 and y1 are approximately x - a*k/2 and y - b*k/2.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, 0, 0
min_dist_sq = float('inf')

# Try all possible k values
for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = b * k
    l = a * k
    
    # Valid x1 range: x1 >= 0, x1 + l <= n, x1 <= x
    x1_max = min(x, n - l)
    if x1_max < 0:
        continue
        
    # Valid y1 range: y1 >= 0, y1 + w <= m, y1 <= y
    y1_max = min(y, m - w)
    if y1_max < 0:
        continue
        
    # For center to be close to (x,y), we want x1 close to x - l/2 and y1 close to y - w/2
    x1_target = x - l / 2
    y1_target = y - w / 2
    
    # Find best x1 in [0, x1_max]
    x1_candidates = []
    if x1_target >= 0:
        x1_candidates.append(int(x1_target))
        if x1_target > 0:
            x1_candidates.append(int(x1_target) - 1)
        if x1_target < x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(int(x1_target) + 1)
    else:
        x1_candidates.append(0)
    
    x1_candidates = [x1 for x1 in x1_candidates if 0 <= x1 <= x1_max]
    
    # Find best y1 in [0, y1_max]
    y1_candidates = []
    if y1_target >= 0:
        y1_candidates.append(int(y1_target))
        if y1_target > 0:
            y1_candidates.append(int(y1_target) - 1)
        if y1_target < y1_max:
            y1_candidates.append(int(y1_target) + 1)
    else:
        y1_candidates.append(0)
        
    y1_candidates = [y1 for y1 in y1_candidates if 0 <= y1 <= y1_max]
    
    # Try all combinations
    for x1 in x1_candidates:
        for y1 in y1_candidates:
            x2 = x1 + l
            y2 = y1 + w
            if x2 > n or y2 > m:
                continue
            center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
            center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
            dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
            
            if dist_sq < min_dist_sq:
                min_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
            elif dist_sq == min_dist_sq:
                # Lexicographic comparison
                current = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
                best_current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                if current < best_current:
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, 0, 0
min_dist_sq = float('inf')

for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = b * k
    l = a * k
    
    x1_max = min(x, n - l)
    if x1_max < 0:
        continue
        
    y1_max = min(y, m - w)
    if y1_max < 0:
        continue
        
    # Find optimal x1 and y1 within bounds
    x1_target = x - l / 2
    y1_target = y - w / 2
    
    # Check candidate values for x1
    x1_candidates = []
    for dx in [-1, 0, 1]:
        cand = int(x1_target + dx)
        if 0 <= cand <= x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(cand)
    
    # Check candidate values for y1  
    y1_candidates = []
    for dy in [-1, 0, 1]:
        cand = int(y1_target + dy)
        if 0 <= cand <= y1_max:
            y1_candidates.append(cand)
    
    # Also add boundaries
    x1_candidates.extend([0, x1_max])
    y1_candidates.extend([0, y1_max])
    
    # Remove duplicates and sort
    x1_candidates = list(set(x1_candidates))
    y1_candidates = list(set(y1_candidates))
    
    for x1 in x1_candidates:
        for y1 in y1_candidates:
            x2 = x1 + l
            y2 = y1 + w
            if x2 > n or y2 > m:
                continue
                
            center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
            center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
            dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
            
            if dist_sq < min_dist_sq:
                min_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
            elif dist_sq == min_dist_sq:
                # Lexicographic comparison
                current = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
                best_current = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                if current < best_current:
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output
17 8 86 92### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
1. Contains the point (x, y)
2. Has length-width ratio exactly a:b
3. Is as close as possible to (x, y) in Euclidean distance
4. If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂ - x₁) / (y₂ - y₁) = a/b
   - This means: b(x₂ - x₁) = a(y₂ - y₁)
   - Let w = x₂ - x₁ (width), h = y₂ - y₁ (height)
   - Then: b·w = a·h, so w/h = a/b

2. We can express the dimensions in terms of a common factor:
   - w = a·k, h = b·k for some positive integer k
   - So the rectangle has width a·k and height b·k

3. Constraints:
   - 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n
   - 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m
   - x₂ = x₁ + a·k
   - y₂ = y₁ + b·k

4. We need to find valid values of k and then choose the one that minimizes distance to center.

## Solution Strategy

1. For a given k, the rectangle has dimensions a·k × b·k
2. We need to find valid ranges for x₁ and y₁:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a·k → x₁ ≥ x - a·k and x₁ ≤ x
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b·k → y₁ ≥ y - b·k and y₁ ≤ y
   - x₁ ≥ 0 and x₁ + a·k ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - a·k
   - y₁ ≥ 0 and y₁ + b·k ≤ m → y₁ ≤ m - b·k

3. So x₁ ∈ [max(0, x - a·k), min(x, n - a·k)]
   And y₁ ∈ [max(0, y - b·k), min(y, m - b·k)]

4. For there to be a valid rectangle with this k, we need:
   - max(0, x - a·k) ≤ min(x, n - a·k)
   - max(0, y - b·k) ≤ min(y, m - b·k)

5. The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁ + x₂)/2, (y₁ + y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + a·k)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + b·k)/2) = (x₁ + a·k/2, y₁ + b·k/2)

6. We want to minimize the Euclidean distance from (x, y) to the center:
   - d² = (x - (x₁ + a·k/2))² + (y - (y₁ + b·k/2))²

7. For a fixed k, we want to minimize d² by choosing optimal x₁ and y₁.

## Optimization

For a fixed k, the optimal x₁ and y₁ are:
- x₁ = max(0, x - a·k) if we want to minimize distance, but we need to stay within bounds
- Actually, since we want to minimize distance, we want x₁ as close to x - a·k/2 as possible
- Similarly for y₁

But we need to respect bounds.

## Algorithm

1. Find valid values of k:
   - k must be such that a·k ≤ n and b·k ≤ m
   - k must be such that we can place the rectangle containing (x, y)

2. For each valid k, determine the range of possible x₁ and y₁:
   - x₁_min = max(0, x - a·k)
   - x₁_max = min(x, n - a·k)
   - y₁_min = max(0, y - b·k)
   - y₁_max = min(y, m - b·k)

3. If ranges are valid (x₁_min ≤ x₁_max and y₁_min ≤ y₁_max), calculate the optimal center.

4. For each valid k, calculate the best (x₁, y₁) that minimizes distance.

5. Among all valid k, choose the one that minimizes Euclidean distance, then lexicographically smallest.

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_distance_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Find valid values of k
    # k must be such that a*k <= n and b*k <= m
    # Also need to fit with point (x,y)
    
    # k can range from 1 up to min(n//a, m//b)
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # We also need to be able to place the rectangle around (x,y)
    # For small k, we can always place it
    # For large k, we need to check bounds
    
    # Try different values of k from 1 to max_k
    # But we don't need to try all - we can optimize the search
    
    # We need to find the k that minimizes distance
    # For each k, we want to find the best placement of the rectangle
    
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        # Width and height
        width = a * k
        height = b * k
        
        # Find valid ranges for x1 and y1
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        # Check if valid placement is possible
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, find the optimal (x1, y1) that minimizes distance
        # Distance to center: (x - (x1 + width/2))^2 + (y - (y1 + height/2))^2
        # = (x - x1 - width/2)^2 + (y - y1 - height/2)^2
        
        # To minimize, we want x1 as close to (x - width/2) as possible
        # and y1 as close to (y - height/2) as possible
        
        target_x1 = x - width / 2
        target_y1 = y - height / 2
        
        # Find closest x1 in range [x1_min, x1_max]
        x1_opt = x1_min
        if target_x1 > x1_min and target_x1 < x1_max:
            x1_opt = int(target_x1)
        if x1_opt < x1_min:
            x1_opt = x1_min
        if x1_opt > x1_max:
            x1_opt = x1_max
            
        # Make sure x1_opt is within bounds
        x1_opt = max(x1_min, min(x1_opt, x1_max))
        
        # Find closest y1 in range [y1_min, y1_max]
        target_y1 = y - height / 2
        y1_opt = y1_min
        if target_y1 > y1_min and target_y1 < y1_max:
            y1_opt = int(target_y1)
        if y1_opt < y1_min:
            y1_opt = y1_min
        if y1_opt > y1_max:
            y1_opt = y1_max
            
        y1_opt = max(y1_min, min(y1_opt, y1_max))
        
        # Calculate distance squared
        x2 = x1_opt + width
        y2 = y1_opt + height
        center_x = (x1_opt + x2) / 2
        center_y = (y1_opt + y2) / 2
        dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
        
        # Update best solution if this is better
        if dist_sq < best_distance_sq or (dist_sq == best_distance_sq and 
                                          (best_x1 is None or 
                                           (x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2))):
            best_distance_sq = dist_sq
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x1_opt + width, y1_opt + height
    
    print(int(best_x1), int(best_y1), int(best_x2), int(best_y2))

solve()
```

```python
    # = (x - x1 - width/2)^2 + (y - y1 - height/2)^2

        # Try the floor and ceiling, pick the one closer to target
        x1_candidates = []
        if target_x1 - x1_min < x1_max - target_x1:
            x1_candidates.append(int(target_x1))
        x1_candidates.append(x1_min)
        x1_candidates.append(x1_max)
        
        # Pick the one closest to target_x1
        x1_opt = min(x1_candidates, key=lambda val: abs(val - target_x1))
        if x1_opt < x1_min or x1_opt > x1_max:
            x1_opt = x1_min
    else:
        x1_opt = x1_min
        
    # Find the closest y1 in range [y1_min, y1_max]
    y1_opt = y1_min
    if target_y1 >= y1_min and target_y1 <= y1_max:
        y1_candidates = []
        if target_y1 - y1_min < y1_max - target_y1:
            y1_candidates.append(int(target_y1))
        y1_candidates.append(y1_min)
        y1_candidates.append(y1_max)
        
        # Pick the one closest to target_y1
        y1_opt = min(y1_candidates, key=lambda val: abs(val - target_y1))
        if y1_opt < y1_min or y1_opt > y1_max:
            y1_opt = y1_min
    else:
        y1_opt = y1_min
    
    # Validate bounds
    x1_opt = max(x1_min, min(x1_opt, x1_max))
    y1_opt = max(y1_min, min(y1_opt, y1_max))
    
    x2 = x1_opt + width
    y2 = y1_opt + height
    
    # Calculate distance squared
    center_x = (x1_opt + x2) / 2
    center_y = (y1_opt + y2) / 2
    dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
    
    # Update best solution
    if dist_sq < best_distance_sq or (dist_sq == best_distance_sq and 
                                     (x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)):
        best_distance_sq = dist_sq
        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2

print(int(best_x1), int(best_y1), int(best_x2), int(best_y2))
```

# Find valid values of k
max_k = min(n // a, m // b)

for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    width = a * k
    height = b * k
    
    # Find valid ranges for x1 and y1
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    # Check if valid placement is possible
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
        
    # For this k, find the optimal (x1, y1) that minimizes distance
    # Distance to center: (x - (x1 + width/2))^2 + (y - (y1 + height/2))^2
    # = (x - x1 - width/2)^2 + (y - y1 - height/2)^2
    
    # To minimize, we want x1 as close to (x - width/2) as possible
    # and y1 as close to (y - height/2) as possible
    
    target_x1 = x - width / 2
    target_y1 = y - height / 2
    
    # Find the closest x1 in range [x1_min, x1_max]
    x1_opt = x1_min
    if target_x1 >= x1_min and target_x1 <= x1_max:
        # Try the floor and ceiling, pick the one closer to target
        x1_candidates = []
        if target_x1 - x1_min < x1_max - target_x1:
            x1_candidates.append(int(target_x1))
        x1_candidates.append(x1_min)
        x1_candidates.append(x1_max)

            x1_opt = x1_min
    else:
        x1_opt = x1_min

        y1_candidates = []
        if target_y1 - y1_min < y1_max - target_y1:
            y1_candidates.append(int(target_y1))
        y1_candidates.append(y1_min)
        y1_candidates.append(y1_max)

    y1_opt = max(y1_min, min(y1_opt, y1_max))
