Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output
17 8 86 92### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
   - Maximizes the area (which means maximizing both width and height)
   - If multiple solutions, choose the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If still tied, choose lexicographically minimum

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂ - x₁) / (y₂ - y₁) = a/b
2. This means: b(x₂ - x₁) = a(y₂ - y₁)
3. We can write: (x₂ - x₁) = k*a and (y₂ - y₁) = k*b for some positive integer k
4. So the width is k*a and height is k*b

## Approach

1. Since we want maximum area, we want to maximize k
2. The constraints are:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂, so x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k*a → x₁ ≥ x - k*a and x₁ ≤ x
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂, so y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k*b → y₁ ≥ y - k*b and y₁ ≤ y
   - x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ ≤ n → x₁ ≥ 0 and x₁ + k*a ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - k*a
   - y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ ≤ m → y₁ ≥ 0 and y₁ + k*b ≤ m → y₁ ≤ m - k*b

3. For a given k, we need:
   - x₁ ≥ max(0, x - k*a)
   - x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k*a)
   - y₁ ≥ max(0, y - k*b)
   - y₁ ≤ min(y, m - k*b)
   - The intervals for x₁ and y₁ must overlap

4. The maximum possible k is limited by:
   - k ≤ n/a (from x₂ ≤ n)
   - k ≤ m/b (from y₂ ≤ m)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Find the maximum possible k such that there exists a valid sub-rectangle
2. For that k, find the optimal position that minimizes distance to (x,y)
3. If multiple solutions exist, choose lexicographically smallest

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Maximum possible k (scaling factor)
    # k*a must fit in width (so k <= n/a)
    # k*b must fit in height (so k <= m/b)
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # If no valid k exists, we need to check smaller k
    # Actually, k=0 is always possible but gives invalid rectangle
    # We need k ≥ 1 since we need positive width and height
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible k from 1 to max_k
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Constraints for x1:
        # x1 <= x (since x <= x2 = x1 + width)
        # x1 >= x - width (since x >= x1)
        # x1 >= 0
        # x1 <= n - width (since x2 = x1 + width <= n)
        
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        # Constraints for y1:
        # y1 <= y (since y <= y2 = y1 + height)
        # y1 >= y - height (since y >= y1)
        # y1 >= 0
        # y1 <= m - height (since y2 = y1 + height <= m)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        # Check if valid rectangle exists for this k
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            # Find the optimal position for this k
            # We want to minimize Euclidean distance from (x,y) to center
            # Center is at ((x1+x2)/2, (y1+y2)/2) = ((x1 + x1 + width)/2, (y1 + y1 + height)/2)
            # = (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
            
            # Distance squared: (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
            
            # We want to minimize this distance
            
            # For x1: we want x1 + width/2 close to x
            # So x1 close to x - width/2
            
            x1_opt = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, x - width/2))
            
            # Adjust to integer boundaries
            x1_candidate = int(x1_opt)
            if x1_candidate < x1_min:
                x1_candidate = x1_min
            elif x1_candidate > x1_max:
                x1_candidate = x1_max
            
            # For y1: we want y1 + height/2 close to y
            # So y1 close to y - height/2
            
            y1_opt = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, y - height/2))
            
            y1_candidate = int(y1_opt)
            if y1_candidate < y1_min:
                y1_candidate = y1_min
            elif y1_candidate > y1_max:
                y1_candidate = y1_max
            
            # We might need to check neighbors to get the best distance
            # Check the closest positions in the valid range
            
            best_x1_k, best_y1_k = None, None
            min_dist_sq = float('inf')
            
            # Check all integer points in the valid range
            # But we only need to check a few candidates
            x1_candidates = [x1_min, x1_max]
            if x1_min < x1_max:
                x1_candidates.append(x1_min + 1)
                x1_candidates.append(x1_max - 1)
            
            y1_candidates = [y1_min, y1_max]
            if y1_min < y1_max:
                y1_candidates.append(y1_min + 1)
                y1_candidates.append(y1_max - 1)
            
            # Add the optimal values we found
            x1_candidates.extend([x1_candidate])
            y1_candidates.extend([y1_candidate])
            
            # Remove duplicates and filter valid ones
            x1_candidates = list(set(x1_candidates))
            y1_candidates = list(set(y1_candidates))
            
            for x1_val in x1_candidates:
                if not (x1_min <= x1_val <= x1_max):
                    continue
                for y1_val in y1_candidates:
                    if not (y1_min <= y1_val <= y1_max):
                        continue
                    
                    x2_val = x1_val + width
                    y2_val = y1_val + height
                    
                    # Center of rectangle
                    cx = (x1_val + x2_val) / 2.0
                    cy = (y1_val + y2_val) / 2.0
                    
                    dist_sq = (cx - x)**2 + (cy - y)**2
                    
                    if dist_sq < min_dist_sq:
                        min_dist_sq = dist_sq
                        best_x1_k, best_y1_k = x1_val, y1_val
                        best_x2_k, best_y2_k = x2_val, y2_val
            
            # If we found a valid solution for this k, check if it's better than current best
            if best_x1_k is not None:
                cx = (best_x1_k + best_x2_k) / 2.0
                cy = (best_y1_k + best_y2_k) / 2.0
                dist_sq = (cx - x)**2 + (cy - y)**2
                
                if dist_sq < best_distance_squared:
                    best_distance_squared = dist_sq
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = best_x1_k, best_y1_k, best_x2_k, best_y2_k
                elif dist_sq == best_distance_squared:
                    # If same distance, choose lexicographically minimum
                    current = (best_x1_k, best_y1_k, best_x2_k, best_y2_k)
                    new = (best_x1_k, best_y1_k, best_x2_k, best_y2_k)
                    # Actually same, so we need to check if we can improve lexicographically
                    pass
    
    # If we didn't find any solution, try smaller k
    # But k=0 is invalid, so minimum k is 1
    
    # If no valid rectangle found, return a valid one
    if best_x1 is None:
        # Try k=1
        k = 1
        width = a
        height = b
        
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            x1 = x1_min
            y1 = y1_min
            x2 = x1 + width
            y2 = y1 + height
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

solve()
```

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating this. Let me simplify and be more systematic:

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Maximum possible scaling factor k
max_k = min(n // a, m // b)

best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
best_dist_sq = float('inf')

# Try all possible k from 1 to max_k
for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    width = k * a
    height = k * b
    
    # Valid range for x1
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    # Valid range for y1
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    # If no valid position exists for this k
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
    
    # For this k, find the best (x1, y1) to minimize distance to center
    # Center is at ((x1 + x1 + width)/2, (y1 + y1 + height)/2) = (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
    # Distance to (x,y): (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
    
    # Try all possible integer positions in valid ranges
    # We only need to check the values that minimize the distance
    
    # For x1: we want x1 ≈ x - width/2
    x1_opt = x - width/2
    x1_candidate = int(x1_opt)
    
    valid_x1_list = []
    for dx in [-1, 0, 1]:
        x1_val = x1_min + dx
        if x1_min <= x1_val <= x1_max:
            valid_x1_list.append(x1_val)
    
    valid_y1_list = []
    for dy in [-1, 0, 1]:
        y1_val = y1_min + dy
        if y1_min <= y1_val <= y1_max:
            valid_y1_list.append(y1_val)
    
    # Also include the bounds
    valid_x1_list.extend([x1_min, x1_max])
    valid_y1_list.extend([y1_min, y1_max])
    
    # Remove duplicates and sort
    valid_x1_list = sorted(set(valid_x1_list))
    valid_y1_list = sorted(set(valid_y1_list))
    
    # Check all combinations
    for x1_val in valid_x1_list:
        for y1_val in valid_y1_list:
            x2_val = x1_val + width
            y2_val = y1_val + height
            
            # Check if it's within bounds
            if x1_val < 0 or x2_val > n or y1_val < 0 or y2_val > m:
                continue
                
            cx = (x1_val + x2_val) / 2.0
            cy = (y1_val + y2_val) / 2.0
            
            dist_sq = (cx - x)**2 + (cy - y)**2
            
            if dist_sq < best_dist_sq:
                best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_val, y1_val, x2_val, y2_val
            elif dist_sq == best_dist_sq:
                # If same distance, choose lexicographically minimum
                current = (x1_val, y1_val, x2_val, y2_val)
                new = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                if current < new:
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_val, y1_val, x2_val, y2_val

if best_x1 is None:
    # Fallback - should not happen given constraints
    # Try k=1
    x1, y1 = 0, 0
    x2, y2 = a, b
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
   - Maximizes the area (or equivalently, maximizes the dimensions)
   - Is closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance to center
   - Is lexicographically minimum if tie

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂ - x₁)/(y₂ - y₁) = a/b
   This means: b(xi - x₁) = a(y₂ - y₁) for some integers

2. Let's define:
   - width = w = x₂ - x₁
   - height = h = y₂ - y₁
   - So w/h = a/b, which means w = k×a and h = k×b for some positive integer k

3. For a fixed k, we have:
   - w = k×a, h = k×b
   - We need: 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n, 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m
   - So: x₁ ≥ 0, x₁ ≤ x, x₁ ≥ x - w, x₂ = x₁ + w ≤ n
   - So: x₁ ≥ max(0, x - w), x₁ ≤ min(x, n - w)
   - Similarly: y₁ ≥ max(0, y - h), y₁ ≤ min(y, m - h)

4. To maximize the area, we want to maximize k (since area = w×h = k²×a×b)

5. The maximum possible k is limited by:
   - k ≤ n/a (from x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ ≤ n)
   - k ≤ m/b (from y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ ≤ m)
   - k ≤ (x - 0)/a (when x is at left edge)
   - k ≤ (n - x)/a (when x is at right edge)
   - k ≤ (y - 0)/b (when y is at bottom edge)
   - k ≤ (m - y)/b (when y is at top edge)

## Algorithm

1. Calculate the maximum possible k based on the constraints
2. For this maximum k, find all valid (x₁, y₁) pairs
3. For each valid rectangle, calculate the center and Euclidean distance
4. Choose the one with minimum distance, then lexicographically minimum

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # The ratio condition: w/h = a/b, so w = k*a, h = k*b
    # We want to maximize k first
    
    # Maximum possible k from x-direction constraints
    # w = k*a, so k <= (n - x) / a when x is at left edge
    # k <= (x - 0) / a when x is at right edge
    # So k <= min(n, x) / a from x-direction
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # More precise constraints from the point (x,y) being inside
    # For x-direction: x₁ >= 0, x₁ <= x, x₁ >= x - w = x - k*a, x₂ = x₁ + k*a <= n
    # So x₁ >= max(0, x - k*a), x₁ <= min(x, n - k*a)
    # For this to be valid: max(0, x - k*a) <= min(x, n - k*a)
    
    # For y-direction: y₁ >= 0, y₁ <= y, y₁ >= y - h = y - k*b, y₂ = y₁ + k*b <= m
    # So y₁ >= max(0, y - k*b), y₁ <= min(y, m - k*b)
    
    # Find maximum k such that there exists valid rectangle
    k_max = 0
    
    # Try from largest possible k downwards to find maximum valid k
    # But we need to be smart about the range
    
    # Maximum k is bounded by min(n//a, m//b)
    k_max = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # But we also need to ensure that there's space around (x,y)
    k_max = min(k_max, x // a)  # x can't go beyond x in x direction
    k_max = min(k_max, (n - x) // a)  # x can't go beyond n in x direction
    k_max = min(k_max, y // b)  # y can't go beyond y in y direction
    k_max = min(k_max, (m - y) // b)  # y can't go beyond m in y direction
    
    if k_max == 0:
        # No valid rectangle possible
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # Now k_max is the maximum k that allows a valid rectangle
    k = k_max
    
    # For this k, find valid x1 and y1
    # x1 must satisfy: max(0, x - k*a) <= x1 <= min(x, n - k*a)
    # y1 must satisfy: max(0, y - k*b) <= y1 <= min(y, m - k*b)
    
    x1_min = max(0, x - k * a)
    x1_max = min(x, n - k * a)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - k * b)
    y1_max = min(y, m - k * b)
    
    # If no valid range, there's an error
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # This shouldn't happen given our k_max calculation
        k = k_max - 1
        x1_min = max(0, x - k * a)
        x1_max = min(x, n - k * a)
        y1_min = max(0, y - k * b)
        y1_max = min(y, m - k * b)
    
    # Find the best rectangle among all valid ones
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # We need to check all valid combinations
    # But note: we want maximum area first (which is achieved by max k)
    # Then closest to (x,y), then lexicographically smallest
    
    # For maximum area, k is already fixed to k_max
    
    # Find all valid (x1, y1)
    for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
        for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
            x2 = x1 + k * a
            y2 = y1 + k * b
            
            # Calculate center of rectangle
            center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
            center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
            
            # Distance from (x,y) to center
            dist_squared = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            # Check if this is better
            if (best_dist_squared == float('inf') or 
                dist_squared < best_dist_squared or 
                (dist_squared == best_dist_squared and 
                 (best_x1 is None or 
                  (x1 < best_x1 or (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1)))):
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                best_dist_squared = dist_squared
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

solve()
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Ma5termind and Subway are bored of their normal life.They want to do something interesting so that they can enjoy their last sem of college life.As usual Ma5termind comes up with a simple and interesting game.

Ma5termind gives Subway a compressed string. A compressed String is composed of characters and numbers.Every character in a compressed string contains a number. Ex a3b1c2 it represents aaabcc. Other compressed string could be a22b2a1 , z5c2x36. Each number in the compressed string indicates the number of times the corresponding character occurs in the string. The final string is 1 indexed.  Ma5termind wants Subway to sort the compressed string and tell him the kth character in the sorted compressed string.Since subway got speed Ma5termind asks him various such questions.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
3. For each query K, we need to find the K-th character in the sorted string
4. If K is outside the bounds of the final string, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their counts
- Handle numbers that might have leading zeros (like "22" or "36")

### Step 2: Build frequency map
- For each character, count how many times it appears in the expanded string

### Step 3: Sort the characters
- Sort characters alphabetically since that's the natural lexicographic order

### Step 4: Answer queries efficiently
- Instead of constructing the entire string (which can be up to 10^18 characters), we can simulate the process
- For each query K, we need to find which character appears at position K
- We can do this by iterating through sorted characters and checking if K falls within their total count

### Step 5: Handle edge cases
- When K exceeds the total length, return -1

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number following the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Add to frequency map
        if char in freq:
            freq[char] += count
        else:
            freq[char] = count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq.keys())
    
    # Calculate total length of the final string
    total_length = sum(freq.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # If k exceeds total length, return -1
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character is at position k
            current_pos = 0
            for char in sorted_chars:
                count = freq[char]
                if current_pos + count >= k:
                    # The k-th character is this character
                    print(char)
                    break
                else:
                    current_pos += count
            else:
                # This should never happen since k <= total_length
                print(-1)

solve()
```

## How it works:

1. **Parsing**: We iterate through the compressed string, extracting each character and its count (which may have multiple digits).

2. **Frequency Map**: We build a dictionary that maps each character to its total count.

3. **Sorting**: We sort the characters alphabetically to get the lexicographic order.

4. **Query Processing**: For each query K:
   - If K exceeds the total length, return -1
   - Otherwise, iterate through sorted characters and find which character contains the K-th position

5. **Efficiency**: This approach is O(Q × C log C) where C is the number of unique characters, which is much better than building the full string (which could be 10^18 characters).

The solution handles the constraint that the final string can be very long (up to 10^18) by avoiding actual string construction and instead using arithmetic to find the K-th character.

    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:

        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number following the character
        num_str = ""

            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Add to frequency map
        if char in freq:
            freq[char] += count
        else:
            freq[char] = count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq.keys())
    
    # Calculate total length of the final string
    total_length = sum(freq.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # If k exceeds total length, return -1
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character is at position k
            current_pos = 0
            for char in sorted_chars:
                count = freq[char]
                if current_pos + count >= k:
                    # The k-th character is this character
                    print(char)
                    break
                else:
                    current_pos += count
            else:
                # This should never happen since k <= total_length
                print(-1)

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Ma5termind and Subway are bored of their normal life.They want to do something interesting so that they can enjoy their last sem of college life.As usual Ma5termind comes up with a simple and interesting game.

Ma5termind gives Subway a compressed string. A compressed String is composed of characters and numbers.Every character in a compressed string contains a number. Ex a3b1c2 it represents aaabcc. Other compressed string could be a22b2a1 , z5c2x36. Each number in the compressed string indicates the number of times the corresponding character occurs in the string. The final string is 1 indexed.  Ma5termind wants Subway to sort the compressed string and tell him the kth character in the sorted compressed string.Since subway got speed Ma5termind asks him various such questions.

Help Subway in answering Ma5termind's question.

Input:

The first line contains the compressed string.This is followed by Q that indicates the number of questions Ma5termind shoots on Subway.Follows Q lines each line contain an integer K.

For every question output the Kth  character if it exists else print -1 .

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to parse the compressed string to get character-frequency pairs
3. Sort the characters by their frequency (and alphabetically for same frequency)
4. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted final string
5. If K is beyond the length of the final string, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Iterate through the string and extract character-frequency pairs
- Handle numbers with leading zeros (like "22" or "36")

### Step 2: Create frequency map
- Store each character with its frequency
- Sort characters by frequency (descending) and then alphabetically (ascending)

### Step 3: Answer queries
- For each query K, we need to find which character is at position K
- We can precompute prefix sums of frequencies to efficiently answer range queries

### Step 4: Handle edge cases
- K exceeds the total length of the final string → return -1

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string into character-frequency map
    char_freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number following the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        freq = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        char_freq[char] = freq
    
    # Create sorted list of characters by frequency (descending) and then alphabetically (ascending)
    # We'll sort by frequency first (descending), then by character (ascending)
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_freq.items(), key=lambda x: (-x[1], x[0]))
    
    # Calculate prefix sums of frequencies for efficient query answering
    prefix_sum = []
    total_length = 0
    
    for char, freq in sorted_chars:
        total_length += freq
        prefix_sum.append((total_length, char))
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is within bounds
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character corresponds to position k
            for length, char in prefix_sum:
                if k <= length:
                    print(char)
                    break

solve()
```

## How it works with the sample:

Input: "a2b3c2a1"
- Parse: a→2, b→3, c→2, a→1
- Character frequencies: a→3, b→3, c→2
- After sorting by frequency (descending) and then alphabetically: (a,3), (b,3), (c,2)
- Prefix sums: (3,a), (6,b), (8,c)
- Final string: "aaabbbcc" (1-indexed)

Queries:
- K=2: falls in range [1,3] → answer is 'a'
- K=5: falls in range [1,6] → answer is 'b' 
- K=1: falls in range [1,3] → answer is 'a'
- K=8: falls in range [1,8] → answer is 'c'

All match the expected output!

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which means "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the characters in the final string and answer K-th queries
3. The final string is sorted lexicographically (alphabetically)
4. We need to handle large K values (up to 10^18) efficiently

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the Compressed String
- Iterate through the string and extract characters and their counts
- Handle numbers that might have multiple digits (like "22")

### Step 2: Generate Character Count Map
- Create a dictionary to store each character and its frequency
- Since we need to sort the final string, we'll sort characters alphabetically

### Step 3: Answer Queries Efficiently
- For each query K, we need to find the K-th character in the sorted string
- Instead of generating the entire string (which could be huge), we can use binary search or direct counting
- We'll maintain the sorted list of (character, count) pairs
- For a given K, iterate through the sorted characters and find which character contains the K-th position

### Step 4: Handle Edge Cases
- If K is larger than the total length of the final string, return -1

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed_string = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character counts
    char_count = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed_string)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed_string[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number following the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed_string[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed_string[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Add to character count
        if char in char_count:
            char_count[char] += count
        else:
            char_count[char] = count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_count.keys())
    
    # Get the total length of the final string
    total_length = sum(char_count.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # If k is larger than total length, return -1
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find the k-th character in sorted order
        current_pos = 0
        
        for char in sorted_chars:
            count = char_count[char]
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # The k-th character is within this character's group
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen as we checked k <= total_length
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Algorithm

1. **Parsing**: We iterate through the compressed string character by character. When we encounter a character, we extract all following digits to form the count.

2. **Character Counting**: We maintain a dictionary to store how many times each character appears in the final string.

3. **Sorting**: We sort the characters alphabetically since the final string is 1-indexed and sorted lexicographically.

4. **Query Processing**: For each query K:
   - If K exceeds the total length, return -1
   - Otherwise, iterate through sorted characters and check if K falls within the range of the current character's count
   - The first character whose count range includes K is our answer

## Time Complexity
- O(N + Q log C) where N is length of compressed string, C is number of unique characters
- Parsing: O(N)
- Sorting: O(C log C) where C ≤ 26 for lowercase letters
- Each query: O(C)

## Space Complexity
- O(C) for storing character counts

The solution efficiently handles the large constraint of K up to 10^18 by avoiding generating the entire string and instead using direct counting.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string (alphabetically) and answer Q queries about the Kth character
3. The final string can be very long (up to 10^18 characters), so we can't generate the entire string
4. We need to efficiently answer each query without constructing the full string

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers that might have leading zeros (like "a22b2a1")

### Step 2: Sort the characters
- Sort by character (alphabetically)
- This gives us the final sorted order

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- For each query K, we need to find which character is at position K
- We can calculate the cumulative frequency of characters
- Since the string is sorted, characters appear in alphabetical order

### Step 4: Handle edge cases
- If K is greater than the total length of the string, return -1

## Implementation Strategy

1. Parse the input string to get character frequencies
2. Sort characters by their alphabet order
3. For each query K, find which character it falls into by checking cumulative frequencies

```python
def solve():
    # Read compressed string
    compressed = input().strip()
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number following the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Add to frequency dictionary
        if count > 0:
            char_freq[char] = char_freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_freq.keys())
    
    # Calculate cumulative frequencies
    cumulative_freq = []
    total_length = 0
    
    for char in sorted_chars:
        freq = char_freq[char]
        cumulative_freq.append((char, freq))
        total_length += freq
    
    # Process queries
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is within bounds
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character contains the Kth position
            current_pos = 0
            for char, freq in cumulative_freq:
                if current_pos + freq >= k:
                    print(char)
                    break
                current_pos += freq
            else:
                # This should not happen given our bounds check
                print(-1)

solve()
```

## Verification with Sample

- Frequencies: a→3, b→3, c→2
- Sorted: a(3), b(3), c(2)
- Total length: 8

Queries:
- K=2: falls in first character 'a' (positions 1-3)
- K=5: falls in second character 'b' (positions 4-6)
- K=1: falls in first character 'a' (position 1)
- K=8: falls in last character 'c' (positions 7-8)

Wait, let me recalculate the final string:
- a(3): positions 1,2,3
- b(3): positions 4,5,6  
- c(2): positions 7,8

So:
- K=2: position 2 → 'a' ✓
- K=5: position 5 → 'b' ✓
- K=1: position 1 → 'a' ✓
- K=8: position 8 → 'c' ✓

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
3. For Q queries, we need to find the K-th character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Go through each character and extract the number that follows it
- Build frequency count for each character

### Step 2: Sort characters
- Since we need to find the K-th character, we need to sort the characters alphabetically
- Characters with the same letter will appear consecutively

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Instead of building the entire string (which could be up to 10^18 characters), we can compute the position
- For each character, calculate how many characters come before it in sorted order
- Use binary search or direct calculation to find the K-th character

### Step 4: Handle edge cases
- K might be larger than the total length of the final string
- Need to check if K is valid before answering

## Implementation Strategy

1. Parse the compressed string to get frequency counts
2. Sort the characters by their alphabet position
3. For each query K:
   - Calculate cumulative frequency of characters up to each position
   - Find which character contains the K-th position
   - Return that character, or -1 if K is out of bounds

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_count = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract number that follows the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Update character count
        if char in char_count:
            char_count[char] += count
        else:
            char_count[char] = count
    
    # Create sorted list of characters by alphabetical order
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_count.keys())
    
    # Total length of final string
    total_length = sum(char_count.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is within valid range
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character contains the k-th position
        current_pos = 0
        
        for char in sorted_chars:
            count = char_count[char]
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # K falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen given our constraints
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## How it works:

1. **Parsing**: We iterate through the compressed string, extracting characters and their counts (even with leading zeros).

2. **Sorting**: We get the unique characters and sort them alphabetically.

3. **Query processing**: For each query K:
   - We check if K exceeds the total length (return -1 if so)
   - We iterate through sorted characters, keeping track of cumulative positions
   - When we find a character where the cumulative position + its count >= K, we know K falls within this character's range

4. **Efficiency**: The solution runs in O(N + Q log C) where N is the length of compressed string, Q is number of queries, and C is the number of unique characters. This is efficient even for large K values (up to 10^18) since we don't build the entire string.

The time complexity is efficient enough for the given constraints, and we avoid creating a string of size up to 10^18 by using direct position calculation.

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_count = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract number that follows the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Update character count
        if char in char_count:
            char_count[char] += count
        else:
            char_count[char] = count
    
    # Create sorted list of characters by alphabetical order
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_count.keys())
    
    # Total length of final string
    total_length = sum(char_count.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is within valid range
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character contains the k-th position
        current_pos = 0
        
        for char in sorted_chars:
            count = char_count[char]
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # K falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen given our constraints
            print(-1)

solve()

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the expanded string (lexicographically)
3. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
4. If K exceeds the length of the final string, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their counts
- Handle numbers with leading zeros (like "22")

### Step 2: Generate the final string
- Create a list of (character, count) pairs
- Sort by character (lexicographically)

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since final string length can be up to 10^18, we can't generate the entire string
- Instead, we need to find which character at position K falls into

### Step 4: Algorithm for queries
- For each query K, iterate through characters in sorted order
- For each character, check if K falls within its count range
- If yes, return that character
- If no character can contain K, return -1

## Implementation

```python
def parse_compressed_string(compressed):
    result = {}
    i = 0
    while i < len(compressed):
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract number part
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(compressed) and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        result[char] = result.get(char, 0) + count
    
    return result

def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character counts
    char_counts = parse_compressed_string(compressed)
    
    # Sort characters lexicographically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_counts.keys())
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is valid
        total_length = sum(char_counts.values())
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character contains the kth position
        current_pos = 0
        for char in sorted_chars:
            count = char_counts[char]
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # k falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen given our validation above
            print(-1)
```

- Sorted characters: ['a', 'b', 'c']
- Final string: "aaabbbcc" (length 8)
- Query k=2: a has positions 1-3, so 2 is in a → output 'a'
- Query k=5: a has 1-3, b has 4-6, so 5 is in b → output 'b'
- Query k=1: in a's range → output 'a'
- Query k=8: in c's range → output 'c'

The solution efficiently handles large values of K without constructing the full string.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to decompress the string and sort it lexicographically
3. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted final string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Go through each character and extract the number that follows it
- The number tells us how many times the character should appear

### Step 2: Build the frequency map
- Create a dictionary to store the frequency of each character
- Process the string character by character, keeping track of the number that follows

### Step 3: Sort characters
- Sort the characters lexicographically (a-z)

### Step 4: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the final string length can be up to 10^18, we can't build the entire string
- For each query K, we need to find which character it belongs to
- We can use prefix sums to determine which character sequence contains position K

### Step 5: Implementation approach
1. Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
2. Sort characters by ASCII value
3. For each query K, iterate through sorted characters and check if K falls within their contribution
4. Return the character or -1 if K is out of bounds

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_frequencies = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Add to frequencies
        if count > 0:
            char_frequencies[char] = char_frequencies.get(char, 0) + count
    
    # Sort characters lexicographically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_frequencies.keys())
    
    # Precompute prefix sums for efficient query answering
    prefix_sum = []
    current_pos = 0
    
    for char in sorted_chars:
        freq = char_frequencies[char]
        prefix_sum.append((current_pos, current_pos + freq))
        current_pos += freq
    
    # Answer each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is within valid range
        if k > current_pos:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character segment contains position K
            for idx, (start, end) in enumerate(prefix_sum):
                if start < k <= end:
                    print(sorted_chars[idx])
                    break

solve()
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
3. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers that might have leading zeros (like "a22b2a1")

### Step 2: Generate the sorted string
- Group characters by their frequency
- Sort characters alphabetically
- Calculate cumulative counts to determine which character is at position K

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the final string length can be up to 10^18, we can't generate the entire string
- Instead, we need to find which character lies at position K by calculating cumulative frequencies

### Step 4: Implementation approach
1. Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
2. Sort characters alphabetically
3. For each query K, iterate through sorted characters and check if K falls within the range of that character's occurrence
4. Return the corresponding character, or -1 if K is out of bounds

```python
def parse_compressed_string(compressed):
    char_freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        if count > 0:
            char_freq[char] = char_freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    return char_freq

def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_freq = parse_compressed_string(compressed)
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_freq.keys())
    
    # Calculate cumulative frequencies
    cumulative_freq = []
    total_length = 0
    
    for char in sorted_chars:
        freq = char_freq[char]
        total_length += freq
        cumulative_freq.append((char, freq, total_length))
    
    # Answer queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is within bounds
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character contains the Kth position
            for char, freq, cumulative in cumulative_freq:
                if k <= cumulative:
                    print(char)
                    break

solve()
```

## Algorithm Explanation

1. **Parse**: Extract each character and its frequency by scanning through the string
2. **Sort**: Sort the characters alphabetically so we process them in the correct order
3. **Cumulative**: Calculate the running total of frequencies to know where each character starts
4. **Query**: For each query K, check if it falls within any character's range

## Time Complexity
- O(N + Q log C) where N is length of compressed string, Q is number of queries, and C is number of unique characters
- Parsing: O(N)
- Sorting: O(C log C) where C ≤ 26 for lowercase letters
- Queries: O(Q log C) in worst case

## Space Complexity
- O(C) for storing character frequencies and sorted list

The solution handles the large constraint of up to 10^18 by not generating the entire string, but instead using mathematical lookup to find the Kth character.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        if N == 1:
            print(0)
        else:
            print((N - 1) % MOD)

solve()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have all 2^N N-digit binary strings
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts the number of adjacent "00" pairs
3. ZeroShark returns True only when there's exactly one "00" pair
4. We need to count how many strings have exactly one "00" pair

## Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:
- N=2: Strings are "00", "01", "10", "11"
  - "00": has one "00" pair → True
  - "01": no "00" pairs → False
  - "10": no "00" pairs → False
  - "11": no "00" pairs → False
  - Answer: 1

- N=3: Strings are all 3-digit combinations
  - "000": has two "00" pairs (positions 0-1 and 1-2) → False (need exactly one)
  - "001": has one "00" pair (positions 0-1) → True
  - "010": no "00" pairs → False
  - "100": has one "00" pair (positions 1-2) → True
  - "011": no "00" pairs → False
  - "101": no "00" pairs → False
  - "110": no "00" pairs → False
  - "111": no "00" pairs → False
  - Answer: 2

## Key Insight

For a string to have exactly one "00" pair, we need exactly one occurrence of consecutive zeros.

Let me think about possible positions:
- If the "00" pair starts at position i, it occupies positions i and i+1
- This means we have a block of at least 2 consecutive zeros starting at position i
- We need exactly one such block

Two cases:
1. A block of exactly 2 zeros (00) with all other positions non-zero
2. A block of more than 2 zeros (like 000) - this would contain multiple "00" pairs

Wait, let me reconsider: if we have "000", positions 0-1 and 1-2 both contain "00" pairs, so it has 2 pairs. So we need exactly one "00" pair.

For a block of exactly 2 consecutive zeros:
- We can place it starting at position 0, 1, 2, ..., n-2
- For each starting position i (where 0 ≤ i ≤ n-2), we have:
  - Positions i and i+1 are '0'
  - All other positions are '1' (to avoid creating additional "00" pairs)

But if we set position i-1 to '0' when i > 0, we might create another "00" pair.

Actually, let me think more systematically:

For exactly one "00" pair:
- We need a block of exactly 2 consecutive zeros
- All other positions must be 1
- This prevents any other "00" pairs from forming

So we place a "00" block at position i (i from 0 to n-2):
- Positions i and i+1 are '0'
- All other positions are '1'

Number of such strings = number of valid starting positions = n-1

But wait, let's check with examples:
- N=2: n-1 = 1 ✓
- N=3: n-1 = 2 ✓

This matches! 

For N=3: positions 0 and 1 → "001", positions 1 and 2 → "100"
This gives us exactly two strings, which matches.

## Edge Cases

What about N=1?
- No possible "00" pair since we need at least 2 positions
- Answer should be 0
- Formula: n-1 = 0 ✓

## Final Solution

For any N ≥ 2, answer is N-1.
For N = 1, answer is 0.

But N-1 works for N=1 too (1-1 = 0).

Therefore, the answer is simply (N-1) mod (10^9+7).

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        result = (N - 1) % MOD
        print(result)

solve()
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have binary strings of length N
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts the number of adjacent pairs "00" in the string (positions i and i-1 where both are '0')

- "01": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → False

- "11": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → False
- Result: 1 string

- "010": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → False
- "100": has "00" at (2,1) → count = 1 → True
- "011": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → False
- "101": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → False
- "110": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → False
- "111": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → False
- Result: 2 strings

## Pattern Recognition

For a string to have exactly one "00" pair:
- The "00" pair can occur at positions (1,0), (2,1), ..., (N-1,N-2)
- For a "00" pair at position (i,i-1), we need:
  - x[i] = '0' and x[i-1] = '0'
  - All other adjacent pairs must NOT be "00"

Let me think about the constraints:

If we have exactly one "00" pair at position (i,i-1), then:
- Positions i and i-1 are both '0'
- For all other j ≠ i-1, j ≠ i, we need to ensure no "00" pairs
- This means:
  - For j < i-1: we can have any pattern, but no adjacent "00" pairs
  - For j > i: we can have any pattern, but no adjacent "00" pairs

Wait, I need to be more careful. Let me reconsider.

Actually, let me think about where the "00" pair can occur:
- It can occur at position (i,i-1) for i from 1 to N-1
- For a "00" pair at (i,i-1), we need:
  - x[i-1] = '0' and x[i] = '0'
  - For all other adjacent pairs (j,j-1) where j ≠ i, we need x[j-1] ≠ x[j] or at least one of them is not '0'

The key insight: for positions before i-1 and after i, we need to avoid "00" pairs.

Let me consider the structure:
- For a "00" pair at position (i,i-1), we have x[i-1] = '0' and x[i] = '0'
- For positions 0 to i-2: we need no "00" pairs
- For positions i+1 to N-1: we need no "00" pairs

For a segment of length k, how many binary strings have no "00" adjacent pairs?

This is a Fibonacci-like sequence:
- For length 1: 2 possibilities ("0", "1")
- For length 2: 3 possibilities ("00" is invalid, so "01", "10", "11") → wait, "00" is invalid
- Actually: "00" has "00" → invalid, so valid: "01", "10", "11" → 3
- For length 3: valid strings with no "00": "001" has "00" → invalid, "010", "011", "100" has "00" → invalid, "101", "110", "111" → valid: "010", "011", "101", "110", "111" → 5

Actually, let me define f(k) = number of binary strings of length k with no "00" adjacent pairs.
- f(1) = 2
- f(2) = 3 (01, 10, 11)
- f(3) = 5 (010, 011, 101, 110, 111)

This follows the Fibonacci sequence: f(k) = f(k-1) + f(k-2)

Now for our problem:

The number of valid strings for a segment of length k with no "00" pairs is f(k), where:

- f(2) = 3


You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

## Algorithm

2. For each query N:

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute Fibonacci numbers up to 100000
    MAX_N = 100000
    fib = [0] * (MAX_N + 2)
    fib[1] = 1
    fib[2] = 1
    for i in range(3, MAX_N + 2):
        fib[i] = (fib[i-1] + fib[i-2]) % MOD
    
    # Precompute g(k) = fib[k+2]
    g = [0] * (MAX_N + 2)
    for i in range(MAX_N + 2):
        if i >= 1:
            g[i] = fib[i + 2] % MOD
    
    # Precompute answers for all possible N
    # ans[n] = sum_{i=0}^{n-2} g[i] * g[n-2-i] for n >= 2, else 0
    ans = [0] * (MAX_N + 2)
    
    for n in range(2, MAX_N + 2):
        total = 0
        for i in range(n - 1):  # i from 0 to n-2
            total = (total + g[i] * g[n - 2 - i]) % MOD
        ans[n] = total
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        print(ans[N])

solve()
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

```python
MOD = 1000000007
```

```python
# Precompute the values for n from 1 to 100000
# After analysis, the pattern is:
# f(1) = 0
# f(2) = 1
# f(3) = 2
# f(4) = 5
# and f(n) = f(n-1) + f(n-2) + f(n-3) might work, but let's try a different one

# The correct recurrence from known combinatorial problems is:
# f(n) = 2*f(n-1) - f(n-2) for n>2, with f(1)=0, f(2)=1
# f(3) = 2*1 - 0 = 2 ✓
# f(4) = 2*2 - 1 = 3 ≠ 5

# I think the answer is actually f(n) = 2^(n-1) - (n-1) for n>=2
# n=2: 2^1 - 1 = 1 ✓
# n=3: 2^2 - 2 = 4-2 = 2 ✓
# n=4: 2^3 - 3 = 8-3 = 5 ✓
# n=5: 2^4 - 4 = 16-4 = 12

# This matches all the values I have!

# So the formula is: f(n) = 2^(n-1) - (n-1) for n >= 2, and f(1) = 0

def solve_n(n):
    if n == 1:
        return 0
    return (pow(2, n-1, MOD) - (n-1)) % MOD

# Precompute for efficiency
max_n = 100000
results = {}
for n in range(1, max_n+1):
    if n == 1:
        results[n] = 0
    else:
        results[n] = (pow(2, n-1, MOD) - (n-1)) % MOD

T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    N = int(input())
    print(results[N])
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have binary strings of length N
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts the number of positions i (from 1 to n-1) where both x[i] and x[i-1] are '0'
3. ZeroShark returns True only when this counter equals exactly 1
4. We need to count how many strings satisfy this condition

## Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**For N=2:**
- "00": positions 1 → x[1]='0', x[0]='0' → counter = 1 → True
- "01": positions 1 → x[1]='1', x[0]='0' → counter = 0 → False  
- "10": positions 1 → x[1]='0', x[0]='1' → counter = 0 → False
- "11": positions 1 → x[1]='1', x[0]='1' → counter = 0 → False

Result: 1 string

**For N=3:**
- "000": positions 1 → (0,0) → counter +=1, position 2 → (0,0) → counter +=1 → counter = 2 → False
- "001": positions 1 → (0,0) → counter +=1, position 2 → (0,1) → counter = 1 → True
- "010": positions 1 → (1,0) → counter = 0, position 2 → (1,0) → counter = 0 → False
- "100": positions 1 → (0,1) → counter = 0, position 2 → (0,0) → counter +=1 → counter = 1 → True
- "011": positions 1 → (1,0) → counter = 0, position 2 → (1,1) → counter = 0 → False
- "101": positions 1 → (0,1) → counter = 0, position 2 → (0,1) → counter = 0 → False
- "110": positions 1 → (1,1) → counter = 0, position 2 → (1,0) → counter = 0 → False
- "111": positions 1 → (1,1) → counter = 0, position 2 → (1,1) → counter = 0 → False

Result: 2 strings ("001", "100")

## Pattern Recognition

The condition is that there is exactly one position i (1 ≤ i ≤ n-1) where both x[i] and x[i-1] are '0'.

This means we have exactly one "00" pair occurring, and no other "00" pairs.

Let me think about the possible cases:

1. **Single "00" at the beginning:** The string starts with "00" and has no other "00" pairs
2. **Single "00" not at beginning:** The string has exactly one occurrence of "00" somewhere in the middle

However, if we have "00" at position i and i+1, we need to ensure no other "00" pairs exist.

Let me consider the structure:
- We need exactly one "00" substring
- This means there are exactly two consecutive zeros somewhere
- All other positions must not have consecutive zeros

## Case Analysis

For a valid string, we need exactly one occurrence of "00" and no other consecutive zeros.

This means:
1. We have exactly one pair of consecutive zeros
2. All other zeros are isolated (not adjacent to another zero)

Final answer: for each N >= 2, the answer is 2^(N-2), and for N=1, it's 0.

```python
MOD = 1000000007

def solve(n):
    if n == 1:
        return 0
    elif n == 2:
        return 1
    else:
        # 2^(n-2) mod MOD
        return pow(2, n-2, MOD)

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    print(solve(n))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have all 2^N N-digit binary strings
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts consecutive pairs of '0's starting from index 1 to n-1
3. We want to count how many strings have exactly one occurrence of "00" in consecutive positions
4. We need to find the length of binTrues (strings where ZeroShark returns True)

## Analyzing ZeroShark Function

ZeroShark(X) returns true when there is exactly one occurrence of "00" in consecutive positions.

For a string of length N, we check positions (1,2), (2,3), ..., (N-1,N) - that's N-1 positions.

We need exactly one "00" pair in consecutive positions.

## Breaking Down the Cases

Let me think about where the single "00" pair can occur:

- The "00" pair can start at position i (0-indexed), where i ranges from 0 to N-2
- If "00" starts at position i, then we have x[i] = '0' and x[i+1] = '0'

For each valid position of the "00" pair, we need to ensure:
1. There is exactly one "00" pair (no other consecutive zeros)
2. All other positions must not have "00" pairs

## Valid Configurations

For a "00" pair at position i:
- Positions i and i+1 must be '0'
- For all positions j < i: we cannot have x[j] = '0' and x[j+1] = '0'
- For all positions j > i+1: we cannot have x[j] = '0' and x[j+1] = '0'

This means:
- Before position i: we can have any valid binary string with no consecutive zeros
- After position i+1: we can have any valid binary string with no consecutive zeros
- At positions i and i+1: must be '0'

## General Pattern

For a valid configuration with exactly one "00" pair:
1. Choose the position i (0 ≤ i ≤ N-2) where the "00" starts
2. For j < i: positions must not have consecutive zeros
3. For j > i+1: positions must not have consecutive zeros  
4. At positions i and i+1: must be '0'

The constraint is that we cannot have any other "00" pairs. This means:
- In the prefix (0 to i-1): no consecutive zeros
- In the suffix (i+2 to N-1): no consecutive zeros

## Counting Valid Strings

For each valid position i of the "00" pair:
- The prefix of length i must have no consecutive zeros
- The suffix of length (N-2-i) must have no consecutive zeros

The number of binary strings of length m with no consecutive zeros is the (m+2)th Fibonacci number.

Let F(m) = number of binary strings of length m with no consecutive zeros:
- F(0) = 1 (empty string)
- F(1) = 2 ("0", "1")
- F(2) = 3 ("01", "10", "11")
- F(m) = F(m-1) + F(m-2)

- F(0) = 1 (empty)
- F(1) = 2 ("0", "1")
- F(2) = 3 ("01", "10", "11") - yes, F(2) = F(1) + F(0) = 2 + 1 = 3
- So F(m) = F(m-1) + F(m-2)

For position i:
- Prefix length = i: valid strings = F(i) for i ≥ 1, F(0) = 1
- Suffix length = N-2-i: valid strings = F(N-2-i) for N-2-i ≥ 1, F(0) = 1
- If i = 0: prefix has length 0 → 1 way
- If i = N-2: suffix has length 0 → 1 way

So total configurations = Σ(i=0 to N-2) F(i) × F(N-2-i)

This is symmetric and equals: F(0)×F(N-2) + F(1)×F(N-3) + ... + F(N-2)×F(0)

This sum is actually the (N)th Fibonacci number in a specific pattern.
